The Headcount sculpture was exactly what Lace and the brief had said: ceramic composite, half a meter long, high-gloss dark glaze, the surface worked with the almost-typographic markings running in bands along its length. Under the clean work lighting it looked less dramatic than it would have under the exhibition lights. More factual.

Vox examined the south face. The reading node at the base: a port cluster in standard provenance format, recessed a half-centimeter into the ceramic, under a dust cap she removed with a thumbnail and set on the table.

The port was clean. Good maintenance. Someone had cared for this object.

"The data architecture," Vox said, setting up her threading cable, "will tell us whether this is a flat file or an active schema. The provenance chain referenced a civic registry format, but that's the outer wrapper. What it wraps decides how you pull it."

"Translation?" Lace asked. She was leaning against the secondary workstation, arms crossed, watching.

"Either it's a document or it's a database." Vox connected the interface to the port. "A document you read once. A database you query."

The handshake took four seconds. Two of the four channels condensed and the freed bandwidth went to the query interface. The data layer began to resolve.

Rook watched Vox work. She read the data as it opened, her attention moving across the display in the same pattern Rook had seen in her workspace — not linear, not random, the logic invisible from outside and plainly there in the results.

The data layer resolved.

Vox went quiet for eleven seconds. That wasn't a normal length of quiet for Vox during a live data operation.

"Fifteen years," she said.

"Of what," Rook asked.

"Migration patterns. Residency histories. Debt ledger cross-references. Heritage documentation filings." Her voice was level; she'd arrived there through the work. "The whole Gray Zone. All twelve zones plus the buffer zones. Population movement, integration rates, financial dependency, ethnographic heritage cross-sections mapped to residency registration."

She paused. "The heritage data is the significant part. It's coded to the civic grid's legacy classification schema — the framework they used before the Meridian Charter harmonized the identity system in 2208. That schema hasn't been used for official civic work in eighteen years."

The room was quiet.

"Who would use it now," Ekow said. Not a question.

Vox turned to him. "Organizations that kept parallel heritage tracking systems when the civic schema was retired. The civic grid stopped, but the schema isn't proprietary — any institution with enough technical capacity could build its own registry against it." She looked back at the screen. "I can count the organizations in the Gray Zone with that capacity on two hands. The Lotus Syndicate is one of them."

The silence stretched.

"The anonymous client," Boiler said.

"Yes," Rook said.

Scout was sitting on the edge of the secondary workstation, legs crossed, watching the ceiling at the angle that meant she was processing. "The Lotus commissioned a recovery job," she said, "to recover a data sculpture holding fifteen years of Gray Zone population data, coded to the heritage classification schema they use for their own registry work." A pause. "They're recovering their own data."

"Or someone stole it from them," Lace said. "And they want it back."

"Tran-Okonkwo bought it from a secondary courier," Ekow said. "He didn't know what it was — he knew it was worth more than market, but not the specific value. So whoever stole it from the original courier didn't know either, or they wouldn't have sold it at secondary-market prices."

Rook was running the chain backward. The sculpture had surfaced at the Z6 secondary market first, then a Z4 holding facility, then Tran-Okonkwo's. Each move was someone who didn't know what they had, selling to someone who also didn't, the value building in the object while nobody in the chain understood why — until Tran-Okonkwo looked at it long enough to understand it was worth waiting for.

And the anonymous client had made two recovery attempts before this one. Two failures in three months. The urgency wasn't market pressure. It was operational.

"Headcount," Vox said. "The title."

Rook looked at her.

"It's a count," Vox said. "Fifteen years of counting heads. Migration patterns are a count. Debt distributions are a count. Heritage cross-sections are a count." She closed two query interfaces and left the heritage layer open on the main display. "This is a census. Not an official one. A parallel census, running the same categories the official system retired in 2208, kept by someone who needed the data those categories produce."

The display showed the heritage cross-section in schematic form: Z6, the buffer zones, every registered residency filing from the last fifteen years, matched against the retired heritage framework. The distribution was visible even in schematic — the concentration maps, the migration flows, residency density by heritage classification across the Gray Zone.

Data woven through the civic grid so quietly, for so long, that it had ended up dressed as art. Somebody had been patient. Fifteen years of patience had made this.

Rook thought about the anonymous client. Φ12,000. Three attempts. Urgent enough to use an outside operator instead of an inside resource. She thought about what an organization with fifteen years of this data and an urgent need to get it back was planning to do with it.

And she thought about Ekow's silence when she'd named the schema at the transit station. The weight behind the pause that had resolved into briefing language without naming itself.

"Vox," she said. "Can you copy the data layer?"

"Already done," Vox said. "First thing on connection. The copy's on isolated storage — not on any networked system." She looked at Rook. "I assumed we'd want options."

"We do," Rook said.

She looked at the case. The sculpture in its foam cradle. Φ12,000 of the anonymous client's property, already copied, data layer open. The Ferris contract specified the physical piece, intact, to a Z3 drop point. It didn't say anything about examining the data layer first. It didn't say what happened if examining it changed the delivery.

"We're not delivering it tonight," she said.

Nobody argued.

"Ferris will need an explanation," Lace said.

"Ferris gets an explanation when I have one worth giving." Rook looked at the display. The heritage cross-section maps. Fifteen years of the Gray Zone's people, counted and sorted and held in ceramic dressed as art.

"What else does the data connect to? What would you specifically need this data for?"

Vox was already running it. "The heritage cross-sections are the key. Migration patterns alone are civic planning data — the Gray Zone authority uses that routinely. Debt distributions are financial profiling, standard market intelligence. But heritage mapping at this resolution, kept against a retired schema for fifteen years..."

She paused. "You'd need this for targeting. Population segmentation. Knowing where specific heritage classifications are concentrated, how they move, and what they owe."

The room held the shape of that for a moment.

Boiler said it out loud. "That's not a census. That's a target list?"

"Yes," Rook said.

She looked at the copied data on isolated storage, the original sculpture in its case, the Φ6,000 sitting in the contract account, and the client's drop coordinates waiting in a file she was going to leave unopened tonight.

The Φ12,000 had been the point for about four hours. Then Vox had opened the data layer, and it stopped being the point, and what replaced it was the question of who had built a target list over fifteen years and what they meant to do with it now that it was nearly complete.

That question didn't have a drop point. It needed a different approach entirely.

She lifted the sculpture from its cradle, turned it once in her hands — the worked surface, the almost-language markings, the ceramic weight of it — and set it carefully back.

It was evidence now.

It was also, she was beginning to understand, only one part of a plan that wasn't finished.

"We sit on the sculpture," she said. "Vox stays on the data. Tomorrow we find out who built this — and what they still need to complete it."

She looked at Ekow. He was looking at the display. The heritage cross-sections. The fifteen years.

He said nothing. His right hand was very still at his side.

Three days after the market run, Adaeze called them in. Adaeze Nnodu-Park's office was exactly the kind of room twelve years of fighting produced: functional, dense with ongoing work, papers in sorted stacks on the second desk, civic filing architecture on the primary system, and the particular smell of a room lived in by someone who didn't stop.

A community legal cooperative that had run twelve years had a certain material reality, and the office wore it — the evidence of cases filed, cases lost, and cases that had produced a record even when they produced nothing else.

She was behind the primary desk when they came in. Early fifties, large frame, the deliberateness of someone who'd learned to move through contested spaces without performing the contest. She looked at Ekow first, Rook second.

"You read the data," she said, to Rook.

"Yes."

"Then you know what Headcount is."

"We know what it contains. We don't yet know what it connects to."

She nodded once, acknowledging the gap and moving past it. "What I'm about to tell you fills the gap. It'll make what you've seen easier to understand and harder to walk away from."

She didn't wait to see whether Rook would object to the framing. She began.

Lee Nari had been a Lotus Stem adjutant for seven years. Not a Stem herself — adjutants were administrative and operational support for the council members who held the votes. The distinction mattered: adjutants sat in the room for most operational briefings and were privy to most operational decisions, but they weren't bloodline members and they didn't vote. They were trusted because they were useful, and useful because they understood the value of the access they'd been given and treated it accordingly.

Nari had been cast out at twenty-eight. The grounds were personal: she'd married outside the Lotus bloodline eligibility rules. Her husband was Emre Farhat-Adeyemi. The marriage hadn't been approved — it had, in fact, been explicitly forbidden under the rules governing bloodline adjutancy. She'd done it anyway. The Stems voted on her status six weeks after she filed the civil registry notice, and the vote wasn't close.

Three months before the vote on her own status, she'd been present in the room for a different vote.

Adaeze delivered this part flat, no editorial. Nari had been present, in her adjutancy role, during a closed Stems session. The format wasn't unusual. The subject was. The Stems had been handed a document the Root had prepared and asked to authorize it. Within the internal Lotus registry, the document was called the Purification Protocol.

The vote had been seven to two in favor.

Rook heard the name. It settled in the room beside the target list in the sculpture, beside the heritage cross-section maps, beside Ekow's particular silence. The three things had a shape together they hadn't had apart.

"Yes," she said — before Adaeze reached the next sentence, because the pattern was already complete. The anonymous client, the fifteen years of demographic data, an unauthorized vote on a document called the Purification Protocol. The next sentence was confirmation of something she already had the shape of.

Adaeze wasn't surprised. She'd spent twelve years fighting through institutional channels for people who couldn't wait for complete information. She knew what it looked like when someone made the call early. She continued anyway. There was more worth having.

Nari hadn't been allowed to take copies of documents from closed sessions. She'd taken a copy of the Protocol anyway, kept it fifteen years in Z6, and waited to decide what to do with it. Now the timeline had compressed. The Lotus had found her location. The Thorns' pattern said a contact team would reach her residential address within twelve hours. Nari had filed a disclosure request with the GLMZ Civic Bureau six days ago. The Bureau hadn't responded.

Adaeze stopped speaking.

The building was quiet around the office the way buffer-zone buildings were quiet in the afternoon — not silent, community work coming up through the floors, the renovation Boiler was running on the east wing, the legal consultation two doors down. Overhead, the faint percussion of structural work: load-bearing interventions, the sound of someone doing something meant to last.

"The Purification Protocol," Rook said. "What specifically?"

"Nari can explain what the document contained," Adaeze said. "I have her summary. The Protocol is a systematic population management initiative targeting non-eligible heritage classifications in the Z6 buffer zone. Phased displacement over a twenty-year timeline. Three operational components required for Phase 1."

Rook waited.

"A demographic baseline," Adaeze said. "Fifteen years of heritage cross-section data."

She didn't say the thing Rook was already thinking. She went on.

"Neuretics registration access. A way to identify and log every registered neuretics user in the Z6 network by heritage classification, in real time. And a deployment window — thirty days from the moment the first two components are in place." She looked at Rook directly. "Nari's summary describes those requirements from the document she read fifteen years ago. The document she has is the authorization record. It has the Stems' signatures."

Ekow hadn't spoken since they came in. He stood near the door — the position he took in every room. He'd been watching Adaeze's face while she spoke, and now he wasn't watching anything, his eyes on a middle distance with nothing in it.

"Twelve hours from the Thorn contact schedule," he said.

"Yes," Adaeze said.

"You got that from pattern analysis, or from inside the schedule?"

She paused fractionally. "Pattern analysis. I don't have inside access to the Thorn operational queue."

He nodded. The nod wasn't about her answer. He was checking something he already knew, running the operational logic he'd been inside of against what she was telling him from outside it. His hands rested at his sides.

"The Thorns use a three-stage contact protocol for a casting-out extraction," he said. Not to Adaeze. To the room.

"First, surveillance confirmation of the current address — they'll have finished that before the clock starts. Then a route feasibility assessment: they map the extraction path from her building and identify alternates. Four to six hours. Then contact deployment — a two-person entry team, assigned before the feasibility assessment is even complete." He paused. "If the pattern analysis read the surveillance confirmation as the start of the twelve-hour window, we're at the end of the route assessment stage. Maybe into early contact deployment."

The room was quieter.

Adaeze looked at him. Her expression hadn't changed much, but her attention had sharpened — not surprise, exactly. The adjustment of someone getting more specific information than she'd expected from the source. "That's a more precise timeline than I had," she said.

"Yes," Ekow said. No elaboration, no apology.

Rook was watching his face. He met Adaeze's gaze with the stillness he brought to everything — the practiced containment of a man who'd sat in rooms where this kind of decision got made, and listened, and known the operational standard from the inside, and hadn't been in a position to use that knowledge until now.

She was aware, at the edge of her operational thinking, of what it meant that he could describe the Thorns' three-stage protocol this precisely.

She set it aside. She'd come back to it.

"Where is Nari now?" Rook asked.

"Her Z6 residential address. She hasn't moved since I spoke with her this morning — moving on her own draws Thorn attention faster than holding position. She has a route I outlined, but she doesn't use it unless I confirm."

"Has the route been walked?"

"Once, by me, three days ago." A pause. "I'm not an operator."

"I know." Rook looked at the desk. The civic filing on the primary system, the sorted stacks on the second desk. Twelve years of fighting Lotus debt structures through institutional channels, two Civic Bureau petitions that had gone nowhere and been filed anyway. The cooperative had survived. Adaeze was still here. Today she'd called in something extra-institutional, which meant the institutional channels had run as far as they were going to run.

"What does Nari know about the Protocol beyond the authorization vote?" Rook asked.

"She knows what the document contained. She knows who authorized it. She knows the operational timeline as it stood fifteen years ago, which may have moved a lot since." Adaeze held her gaze. "She waited fifteen years to use this. She'll tell you what you need to know when she trusts you enough to tell you."

"She'll trust us enough when we've gotten her clear," Rook said.

"Yes," Adaeze said.

Rook looked at Ekow again. He was still at the door. The expression she hadn't been able to read for most of the meeting had settled into something she could place now: not resolution, not readiness — more the moment before a man does a thing he decided on a long time ago and has only been waiting for the conditions to do.

"Ekow," she said. "The south approach to Nari's building. Tell me what you know."

He looked at her directly. "I've used it," he said. "I know where the Thorn coverage has gaps, and I know the route that avoids the staging facility west of her block." He paused. "I know which maintenance access connects to the crawlway network on her floor. I used that access four years ago. The building infrastructure hasn't changed."

The room absorbed it.

"We'll need current Thorn positions in Z6," Rook said — to the team, to Vox on the channel. "The extraction route walked again before we move. And I want to know what the twelve-hour window looks like from the route feasibility stage Ekow just described."

She looked at Adaeze. "Tell Nari we're coming. Don't give her a time. Tell her tonight."

Adaeze nodded — the nod she used when she'd been doing what could be done with the resources she had, a new resource had just arrived, and the calculation had shifted from uncertain to possible. A careful nod. She wasn't a person who performed relief.

"Tonight," she said.

Out in the corridor, Boiler's renovation work went on overhead: the rhythmic percussion of structural intervention in progress. Someone finding what held and what released under pressure. Someone making a thing meant to last.

They moved the briefing to the safe house. Adaeze's building had too much foot traffic for an operational brief and not enough secure comms for what Ekow needed to run. They drove in two vehicles, staggered as before. The team channel stayed quiet during transit — the channel's way of saying there was nothing immediate to say and the silence wasn't a gap.

Ekow had a physical map.

Not standard. Rook had expected him to pull routing data from mesh or ask for Vox's threading architecture. Instead he produced a folded print — a Z6 grid overlay, the kind the civic mapping office generated for infrastructure maintenance, the kind that lived in the permit archives. Not the current revision; the date stamp in the corner was eighteen months old. He'd annotated it by hand in blue marking compound — small, precise notations, the permanent kind that didn't smear or reflect. The annotation layer was dense. A lot of work had gone into this map over a long time.

He spread it on the cleared table. The transport case had been moved to the storage room. The workstation ran Vox's isolated copy of the Headcount data. The map took the center of the surface.

"Z6 and the buffer south," he said, setting his hands at two points to establish the geography. His hands were large and still on the page, the reinforced bone of his combat frame showing in how they rested — not weight, exactly, but density.

"Nari's residential block is here, Z6 eastern sector, four blocks from the buffer boundary." His finger moved east, then traced a corridor.

"The Thorn primary patrol covers this — the arterial and the two cross-streets around it. The cycle runs a forty-minute rotation in the evening, dropping to twenty-five minutes between twenty-two hundred and zero two hundred, when the pedestrian density drops and the activity window opens."

Rook looked at the map. The annotations: blue marks at regular intervals she read, after a moment, as surveillance node positions. Blue brackets around three blocks — safe-house designations. Small asterisks at five points she couldn't immediately place.

"The asterisks," she said.

"Surveillance dead zones. Places where the Thorn coverage overlap fails — either the node placement doesn't account for the building geometry, or the coverage frequency is lower because of the node's maintenance history. If you're moving through Z6 and you don't want to be logged, those are the positions where the gap is reliable."

"You mapped these," Vox said. She was at the workstation, half her attention on the Headcount query.

"Over time," Ekow said. He didn't elaborate.

Boiler was reading the map the way he read structural drawings — methodically, tracing load paths instead of geography. He put a finger on the blue bracket nearest Nari's building. "This one's active?"

"As of four days ago. It runs as a debt-collection admin office during business hours. After twenty-one hundred it's a staging facility. Standard two-person occupancy for contact deployment."

"You know the rotation for that facility specifically," Boiler said.

"Yes."

Boiler looked up from the map. He and Ekow looked at each other a moment. Boiler's face held a question he'd already answered and was deciding whether to speak. He decided not to. He looked back at the map.

Scout was at the workstation edge, watching the map instead of the screen. "The maintenance crawlway network under the Z6 eastern sector," she said. "Still running the pre-2218 civic layout?"

"Yes. The Z6 infrastructure budget hasn't funded a layout update since 2214. The crawlways are the original 2180s installation with patched sections." He moved to the map's lower margin, where a second annotation layer ran parallel to the street grid. "The original layout is here. I've marked the patched sections — they run different access parameters than the original. Your threading equipment handles both, but the access sequence differs between original and patched hardware."

Scout was already keying something into her mesh.

Lace had been quiet since they entered the safe house, standing at the edge of the map's orbit with the expression she wore while running an assessment she hadn't finished. She looked at the annotations — the years of precise blue marking, the dead zones and patrol rotations and safe-house designations — and her assessment resolved into something she was going to say.

"How long have you had this map?" she asked.

"Six years," Ekow said. "Continuously updated."

"Six years of updating an operational map of the Lotus Z6 enforcement infrastructure?" She said it flat, the way she said any fact. "You kept this current while you were running contract jobs for them."

"Yes," he said.

The room was quiet.

Rook was watching his face. He met Lace's gaze without the deflection of a man being accused and without the performance of a man being cleared. He met it with the stillness of someone who'd waited a long time for this moment and had already decided how he'd stand in it. His face said: I kept this map. I know what it contains and what it means that I have it. I'm not asking you to resolve that for me. That's mine to carry.

His right hand was very still at his side.

Lace held his gaze three seconds. Then she looked back at the map. "The dead zone at the northeast corner of Nari's block," she said. "How large is the coverage gap?"

"Reliable for about eight minutes before the sweep cycle corrects," Ekow said. "If you need more than eight minutes there, you get through it before the correction — you don't wait for a second gap."

"Eight minutes is enough for a building entry," Scout said.

"With the right threading sequence, yes." He pointed to the position. "The building has a maintenance access on the north face that connects to the crawlway junction here. That junction is the original 2180s installation — standard access protocol. If the contact team comes from the Thorn staging facility, they'll approach from the west on the arterial. That takes them away from this access point entirely. Your ingress is separate from theirs."

"How long from the staging facility to her building on their standard route?" Rook asked.

"Nine minutes walking. Fourteen if they run surveillance protocol instead of a direct approach."

"They'll run surveillance protocol," she said.

"Yes." A pause. "The contact teams always run surveillance protocol for casting-out extractions. It's in the operational standard. They confirm position and route before they approach." He said it without the emphasis that would have made it anything but a briefing. He'd read the Thorns' operational standard. He'd been next to it for twelve years.

Vox had turned her full attention to the briefing now, the query running in secondary. She was reading the source structure, Rook could tell — not just the data but the way it was organized, what it revealed about how and when it had been acquired.

"The extraction window," Rook said. "Best timing given the patrol cycle."

"Between twenty-two forty-five and zero one hundred," Ekow said. "There's a gap in the secondary patrol rotation — the point where the patrol teams overlap at the west end of the arterial, opening a coverage gap in the eastern sector. It runs about forty minutes. If you're moving Nari from her building to the buffer boundary, you want to cross into the buffer in that window." He looked at Rook. "I can walk the extraction route. I know where the Thorns will be."

The room absorbed that.

He knew the patrol rotations, the dead zones, the staging occupancy, the extraction route, the contact team's standard. He'd been inside it twelve years. He'd kept a current map and updated it continuously while running jobs — but the jobs didn't need a continuously updated map of surveillance dead zones. Whatever needed that map was something he'd been carrying privately for six years.

Nobody said it.

Vox said it, from the workstation, in the flat register she used when a number refused to reconcile.

"The map's eighteen months stale." She hadn't looked up. "Date stamp. But you said the staging facility went active four days ago, and the rotation dropped to twenty-five-minute cycles — that's a current revision detail?"

Now she did look up. "The Headcount source structure. I'm reading the acquisition metadata on the query I isolated. There's a Z6 infrastructure pull four days old, keyed to a maintenance-office credential."

A beat. "Ekow's credential. His contract access. You've been updating this map through the same channel we're extracting Nari across — which means the moment we move, the log shows your credential touched Z6 mapping data days before an unauthorized ingress in the exact sector?"

The room's silence changed quality.

"The Thorns run post-incident correlation on every casting-out that goes wrong," Lace said, quiet, the assessment finishing itself in real time. "They'll pull the access history. Your six years of private accounting is a six-year trail with your name on every entry. The route that keeps us invisible is the route that points at you the day after."

Ekow's still hand didn't move. But something in his face registered it — the thing he'd carried privately for six years was not, it turned out, only his to carry, and the carrying had left prints. "I knew the map could be traced to me," he said. "I didn't account for the acquisition timestamp landing inside the operational window." A pause, level. "That's a real exposure. It's mine, and it's now the crew's."

Rook heard it and let it sit — but the shape of the beat had changed under her, and she felt the extraction plan she'd been assembling stop being clean.

Boiler looked toward the community center's east wing through the safe-house wall, toward the place he'd been renovating for weeks. His face was the one he wore on the scaffold, running calculations.

"The staging facility at the west end of the buffer," he said. "The one marked with a double bracket."

"A community health clinic during the day," Ekow said. "In the evening it runs a debt-ledger operation. Standard Lotus dual-function facility."

Boiler nodded slowly. He looked at the map and didn't say what he was thinking, which was what everyone in the room was thinking: the debt collectors in the clinic building, at the same address as the health service. The surveillance facility in the administrative office. The same institution wearing two faces at different hours without contradiction.

He said, instead: "The load paths in that sector are going to be complicated."

"Yes," Ekow said. And his voice, for the first time in the briefing, had a particular quality — not regret, not apology, something more structural than either. The acknowledgment of a man who'd spent twelve years as part of the load-bearing infrastructure of something that also happened to be this. Not a confession. A reckoning, made quietly, in front of people who'd seen enough to understand it, and over in a sentence.

Rook heard it and let it sit.

"Tonight," she said. "We need the extraction route walked before twenty-two hundred. Ekow, take Scout on the route confirmation. I want current surveillance positions verified against the map, and I want to know which dead zones are still reliable at the hour we'll be using them."

A beat. "Vox — before anything else, I need to know whether that acquisition timestamp can be scrubbed or buried, or whether we plan around it. If it can't move, the route moves."

"Yes," Ekow said.

He folded the map with the care of a man who'd maintained an object for six years and wasn't done needing it. His hands were precise with it. He slid it back into the inside pocket of his field gear, and the gesture had the quality of a thing being carried that was also, in this company, being shared for the first time — not handed over, not surrendered, but no longer carried alone.

"Two hours," Scout said, checking her threading queue. "I'll have the crawlway access sequences loaded before we walk the route."

"Good," Rook said. "Vox, I still need the anonymous client identity. Midnight or earlier."

"Working," Vox said from the workstation. Four channels, streams running. She was already in it.

The safe house organized itself around the operational picture. This was what a crew looked like moving from briefing to working — the air changed, the spaces between people contracted, the silence became the silence of people doing things instead of considering them. Rook had been in enough rooms at this stage to know it by feel.

She looked at the spot on the cleared table where the map had been.

Six years of private accounting. Now this room's. The same load. Different shoulders under it. And a trail underneath it, six years deep, with one name on every line.

Adaeze was still naming the Purification Protocol's vote count when Rook said, "Yes."

She caught up to it afterward, not during. She had agreed somewhere in the middle of the brief and only noticed it once it was done. Adaeze had been three-quarters through. The Civic Bureau had filed nothing, answered nothing, left Nari's disclosure request sitting in a queue. The Thorns had a contact schedule. Twelve hours. A document with seven signatures. Rook had said yes before Adaeze reached the part about what she was actually asking the crew to do.

Adaeze had paused at the word. Not surprised. She had simply heard the decision land before the pitch was finished, and she kept going anyway, because the rest was worth having. Rook listened to it as a person who had already agreed and was learning, now, the exact shape of what she had agreed to.

None of the rest changed her answer.

She sat with that an hour later at the safe house table. Ekow's marked-up map lay under her hands. Vox was running the Headcount query on four channels. The crew was arranged around the cleared room the way they arranged themselves when they were working, not when they were relaxed.

Two threads.

The sculpture sat in the storage room, its data layer copied, the physical piece held. The anonymous client's drop coordinates sat unopened in her neuretics queue. She had checked them twice, confirmed the format, and decided not to read the full coordinates until she made the call on delivery.

She had made it.

They weren't delivering, not until she knew who the client was. That was Thread 1: hold the asset, find the source, decide.

The extraction was Thread 2. Get Nari clear before the Thorn team finished their route work. Get the document out with her. Then decide what to do with that too.

Running both at once was not the problem. The twelve-hour window settled that. The problem was where each person needed to be, how the two threads talked to each other, and what happened if one went wrong while the other was committed.

She walked it through out loud. Ekow on the extraction route — the Z6 knowledge, the south approach, the maintenance access he had used himself four years ago. Boiler as backup on the ground in Z6, the buffer-zone blocks he could move through without a map. Scout in the crawlways and the coverage network, two ways into the building's systems. Lace in reserve for soft cover, the right face in the right hallway if the Thorn approach fouled the exit. Vox on both threads at once. Rook across them.

Six people, two jobs, one source they didn't yet know they shared.

"The sculpture client," Lace said. She was looking at the ceiling, the way she did when she was turning something over. "Anonymous. Φ12,000. Three tries before us. They waited long enough for the piece to move twice." A pause. "They knew it existed and they knew it was in the secondary market. They didn't know exactly where for three months. That's a strange gap for someone who wants a thing badly enough to pay Φ12,000."

"They lost the chain of custody when the courier went to ground," Vox said. "The piece hit the gray market without them knowing. They tracked it through the public feeds, and public feeds run slow."

"Why would anyone with the money for a Φ12,000 recovery not have direct eyes on the Z6 secondary market?" Lace asked. She wasn't asking anyone. She was asking the situation.

Ekow was quiet.

"A clean organization wouldn't," Scout said. "One with an official market presence would have current feeds on Z4 and Z6. The delay means they run their market access off the books, through secondary sources, which lag."

"Or they keep a clean public face and run the off-books work through separate channels that don't share what they know across the line," Lace said.

She said it without looking at Ekow. Nobody looked at Ekow.

The room put it together in silence, which was faster.

The anonymous client for Headcount: Φ12,000, three tries, fifteen years of Gray Zone demographics filed in heritage-classification format. Ekow going quiet when she first named that format at the transit station. Adaeze's brief, and the Protocol's first requirement — a demographic baseline.

The two threads led to one source. Nobody had said it as a confirmed fact. It wasn't on the board yet. But you could see the shape of it, the way you can see a crack that the paint hasn't reached — not the fault itself, just where the wall has been carrying it.

"We need the client's name before we make the connection out loud," Rook said.

"Working on it," Vox said. "The contract wall through Ferris is solid. But whoever set up the drop used a commercial staging address, and those have lease records. It takes time."

"How much?" Rook asked.

"Half a day. Less if the trail's shallow."

She nodded. Half a day was enough to finish the extraction if it ran clean. If they had the name by the time Nari was out, they'd hold both threads at once.

What to do with two threads that led to the same source was a question for after they'd confirmed the source. One problem at a time.

"If we're right," Boiler said, carefully, "then the client wants both what we're holding and Nari kept quiet."

"Yes," Rook said.

He took that in. "So this isn't two jobs with one source. This is us working against our own client on Thread 2."

"We took the job before we knew what it was," she said.

"I know." He wasn't accusing her. He was reading the position, the way he read a wall he had to trust his weight to. "Which means we got paid to work against the people we're now working against."

"Half the contract," she said. "The second tranche isn't paid until we hand over the piece. We're not handing it over."

Boiler looked at the ceiling a moment, then back down. "All right," he said — his way of telling you he'd worked it through and accepted the answer without needing it to be a nicer answer than it was.

Lace unfolded her arms. "The Φ12,000 is gone."

"The Φ12,000 was never the point," Rook said.

"I know. I'm saying it for the record." A pause. "What does the client do when we don't deliver?"

"They go to Ferris. Ferris tells them the delivery's delayed. The contract language gives us enough slack for that hold. They won't lean on Ferris right away — they'll wait. And while they wait, the extraction finishes and Nari's documents start moving through disclosure."

"And if they connect us to the extraction before her documents are filed?" Lace asked.

"They might," Rook said. "We'll be faster."

Lace held that a moment. Then she nodded — not Boiler's structural all right, but something closer to her own.

The nod of a woman who had spent years living inside other people's positions and had just found, in this one, a reason to live inside her own.

Rook looked around the table. Six people who hadn't been in the same room three weeks ago, now sitting around it in the shape of a crew that had already committed.

She thought again about the moment at Adaeze's desk. She had said yes before the brief was done. She had done that before — taken jobs before all the facts were in, worked forward into the shape of a thing instead of waiting for it to resolve. It was an old habit. But lately it had been pointing at different things. At the people in a job, not just the money. At the Reibo flechette she hadn't put down since the last one.

She had agreed before Adaeze finished. She wasn't surprised she agreed. She was a little surprised she wasn't surprised, and she didn't yet know what that pointed at.

She let it sit. There was work to do.

"Two threads," she said. "We start the extraction approach at twenty-two hundred. Vox, I need the client's name by midnight. Ekow, Scout — walk the route once more before then. Confirm every surveillance position."

"Working," Vox said.

"Yes," Ekow said.

"Lace."

She looked up from wherever she'd been.

"When we know who the client is," Rook said, "you'll know what face to wear when we meet them."

Lace smiled — the small real one, the one she didn't put on for anyone. "I'll know before you do," she said.

"Yes," Rook said. "You probably will."

She turned north. Nari's building was two blocks ahead.

Ekow's hand came up before they'd cleared the first storefront — a small motion, palm down, the crew's signal to hold. Rook stopped.

"There's a light in Nari's stairwell," he said. "Second floor. That window's dark every night I've walked this block for twelve years."

Rook followed his eyeline. The building sat quiet at the end of the block, residential, unremarkable — except for the one lit window, cold and steady, on the floor below Nari's unit.

"Could be a neighbor," Scout said, from across the street.

"The unit below Nari's has been empty since the spring," Ekow said. "The cooperative couldn't fill it. No one's paid the lease." A pause. "Someone's in it now."

"Collections?" Lace asked.

"Collections don't sit in the dark," Ekow said. "They come in with the door open and the light on. That's the point of them." He was still watching the window. "Whoever that is doesn't want to be seen from the street. Which means they want to see who comes to the street."

Boiler had gone still again, but it wasn't a wall he was reading this time. "Two blocks of open approach," he said, low. "And someone with a sightline on the only door."

Rook ran it. Primary plan put them at Nari's entrance in ninety seconds along the north face — the face that window looked down on. The market's service network was three blocks back the way they'd come, and getting to it now meant crossing that sightline twice.

The easy exit had just become the only exit, and it was behind them.

"Hold," she said. "No one moves on that door until we know who's watching it."

They moved toward the stairwell.

Then Scout's voice broke the channel, low and fast.

"Hold. Movement on the entry floor. Two — no, three. Not residents. They came in slow and split the moment they cleared the door. That's a search pattern."

Rook stopped.

Behind her, Nari stopped too — no question, no gasp, just the stillness of a person who had rehearsed every version of this except the one where someone was already coming.

"Lace," Rook said. "The exit route."

A beat too long.

"The east crawlway's compromised. There's a second team staged at the maintenance access — they were sitting on it before we got here. Boss, they didn't follow us in. They were already waiting for the route."

Rook's stomach went cold and flat. Someone had known. Not the address — the route. The one Scout had confirmed. The one they'd loaded.

"Boiler," she said. "The stairwell."

"Occupied," Boiler said, and she heard the strain of him already moving. "Two on the landing below me. I can hold them a minute. Not more."

Three years of readiness, a bag packed and a name filed and a chair pointed at the door — and none of it had planned for the door being watched from the other side.

Nari spoke, level as ever, the voice she'd used for *Emre died twelve years ago*.

"They found me. Or they found you coming for me."

A pause.

"It doesn't matter which. We can't use the door, and we can't use the crawlway."

"No," Rook said. "We can't."

The primary route was gone.

Behind them, the photograph stayed on the shelf — a man and a woman at a table, an ordinary Tuesday. The apartment door was closed. The room was as it had been, except for the missing bag by the door.

The approach pattern hit Vox's channel at twenty-three forty-seven.

Rook felt the shift before Vox spoke — the way four-channel intelligence tightened when one channel found something and the others made room. The channel narrowed. Then Vox.

"Thorn contact signature. Three blocks west, moving east on the arterial. Two-person contact team, surveillance protocol."

Two seconds while she ran the route.

"ETA to Nari's building is sixteen minutes if they stay on the arterial. Twelve if they take the cross-street cutoff."

Rook looked at Ekow.

"They'll take the cross-street," he said. "It's the standard approach for the eastern sector."

"Twelve minutes," she said. "Scout."

"I have it," Scout said. She was in the crawlway two levels below the occupied floors, her threading interface deployed, two crawlers live. "I can thread the building's surveillance nodes from here and open a window on the cross-street approach. Ninety seconds."

"Build it," Rook said.

She was on the second floor with Nari, three doors from the maintenance panel that fed the crawlway. The corridor was quiet — Z6 at twenty-three forty-seven, the deep quiet of people asleep in rooms they trusted. The extraction had been orderly until the channel shifted. Path mapped, exit clear, the buffer boundary eleven minutes off at a careful pace.

Eleven minutes if they moved now. Twelve before the contact team reached them.

The margin was thin.

"Boiler," she said.

"West stairwell," he said, one floor down. "I can hear the ground-floor door if it opens."

"If they reach the entrance before we're clear?"

"I know," he said. Nothing more. He didn't need more.

Ekow was already moving toward the stairwell. Not running — the building was asleep, and running woke people, and woken people cost minutes they didn't have. He moved at the pace that covers ground without announcing it.

"East service corridor," he said, turning briefly, voice low. "Off the east stairwell, out to the maintenance access on the north face. If they come in the main entrance, the east corridor takes us clear of their line entirely. Three doors from here."

"Scout," Rook said.

"The east corridor ties into the same crawlway network," Scout said, her voice close and boxed-in, the building's structure audible around it. "I'm at the junction. Move to the east corridor and I can thread you straight to the exterior without touching the main exit."

"The team will sweep the interior once they're in," Vox said. "They'll ping the building's Lotus nodes for recent mesh activity. If Nari's neuretics have been active in the last four hours, the ping confirms her position."

Rook looked at Nari. She stood with the bag on her shoulder, reading the channel off the faces around her — she had the shape of the situation, not the detail, and she wasn't filling the gaps with fear. She was waiting.

"Your neuretics," Rook said.

"Passive-only since Adaeze reached me," Nari said. "No active transmission for six days."

"Passive-only is below the ping threshold," Ekow said, already at the east stairwell door. "The ping looks for active mesh. She won't register." He held the door. "We move."

Rook moved with Nari. Three doors east — the maintenance panel on the left, utility paint, the handle contractors used and residents ignored. She pulled it open. Behind it, a short passage, four meters, then a ladder down into the crawlway. The space was built for a tech and his tools and nothing else.

She went first, down to the base of the ladder, and steadied herself.

Nari didn't hesitate. She shifted the bag to a front grip and came down with the control of someone who was afraid and would not let the fear run her hands. She landed without a sound.

Ekow came down behind her and pulled the panel shut.

"West stairwell," Boiler said, in the channel. "Ground-floor entrance has movement. They're at the door."

"Forty seconds to the junction," Scout said. "Moving."

The crawlway was original 2180s work — two meters of clearance, conduit bundles overhead in iron brackets, junction lights amber at intervals, the air cool with the smell of infrastructure running in the dark under a sleeping building. Forty years of the same job. The brackets steady overhead, the cable runs laid out four decades ago and never changed.

Scout was ahead, one crawler running point, one threading the building's surveillance loop. Rook could see the lead crawler's indicator blinking in the amber light, small and busy, doing its work.

"Team's through the main entrance," Vox said. "West stairwell, ground floor. Running surveillance protocol — pinging the Lotus nodes first, not advancing yet."

Exactly what Ekow had said they'd do.

"Boiler," Rook said.

What Boiler did in the next ninety seconds didn't come across the channel. Rook heard it from below and to the west: the sound of two people running into something that made them stop and deal with it before they could go on. Not a fight. A problem that looked like an accident and worked like a wall. It lasted ninety seconds. Then it was done.

"Two minutes," Boiler said. "I'm on the east stairwell. Coming down."

"Scout," Rook said.

"Exterior access twelve meters ahead," Scout said. "I'm at the hatch. It opens to the service lane on the north face, outside the Thorn patrol corridor. Nearest patrol is at the western arterial crossing." A pause. "I've looped the two corridor nodes on the approach into a passive read. Ambient only, no active traffic."

Twelve meters. Ten. Scout's second crawler on the crawlway floor ahead, indicator blinking. Five. The hatch at the passage end.

Scout was at it, the seals already disengaged — magnetic lock inside, weather seal out, everything set for a one-motion open no louder than a door. She looked at Rook. Three fingers. Two. One.

She opened the hatch.

The north lane was cold and dark and empty, the way Z6 got at twenty-three fifty-three on a weeknight. Residents asleep, the night trade two blocks south, the patrol where Scout had put it. The air came in with the buffer zone's smell — ferrocement and the canal two blocks east and the quiet of a neighborhood hours into sleep.

Rook stepped out. Nari behind her. Scout followed with both crawlers, the equipment retracting. Ekow came last and brought the hatch to closed.

"Boiler," Rook said.

"East stairwell. Thirty seconds."

She waited. The lane, the cold, the sound of a city at rest. The bag on Nari's shoulder, out in the open now — three years of weight, in a lane in Z6, with the patrol at the western arterial and eight minutes of coverage gap ahead.

The east door opened. Boiler came through. He looked at Rook, at the lane, at the way they were going.

"Clean," he said.

"Clean," she confirmed.

She took the lead. The crew arranged around Nari without boxing her in — six people moving through a night city as a group, not a convoy, walking, not running, people with a destination rather than people fleeing one. Ekow fell in at the rear, covering the way they'd come. Scout had the crawlers stowed. Boiler at the right shoulder, Lace at the left.

Eight minutes to the boundary.

"They're doing a floor check," Vox said. "Second floor. They found the apartment."

"And?"

"Door's closed. Passive mesh, no active signatures, no flag on the Lotus node. Her registration reads as a passive-mode resident. They're not reading an absence yet — they're reading someone asleep." A pause. "They'll read it as an absence in about four minutes, when the check gets no answer."

"Four minutes is enough," Rook said.

"Yes," Vox said.

They moved north in the lane, then east at the first junction, the route Ekow had walked twice in the last three hours and, before that, for years in the other direction. The dead zones were exactly where the map said — the northeast corner of Nari's block, eight clean minutes before the sweep corrected, the gaps Ekow had been recording for six years and had never used until tonight.

The city slept around them. The clinics ran. The markets were shut. The debt offices were dark, in the one hour they were dark.

In four minutes, by Vox's count, the team would report the target gone and call for a route search. The search would go to the staging facility three blocks west, which would widen the perimeter to the buffer boundary. The boundary was where the crew would be in four minutes, stepping through at the point Ekow had marked on the map with an asterisk.

Dead zone. Coverage gap. Reliable.

The boundary came exactly when it should have. Ekow held at the last block and looked back once — Old Harbor, the clinic lights, the lane, the building they'd left through its hatch — and then he stepped through.

The crew was across in a spread of forty seconds.

No contact. No alert. The patrol still sat at the western arterial, doing what it had been doing when Scout flagged it seven minutes ago.

Rook didn't stop moving, but she let herself feel the finish — the full breath of a job that went from orderly to pressured and back to controlled without breaking. The crew was good at this. They'd been good at it for three years across three jobs, and they hadn't lost it.

Nari walked in the space Boiler had opened for her, the bag still on her shoulder, her pace matched to the crew without being told. She watched the street ahead like someone who had waited a long time to be walking this street in this direction. She hadn't spoken since the crawlway.

"Two blocks to the vehicle," Vox said.

"We have it," Rook said.

The vehicle was a Vox contact, same as the Z3 run: sealed courier, commercial plates, a driver who'd been waiting and wouldn't ask. It sat at the edge of the transit node, unremarkable among the handful of late-shift vehicles.

Rook put the crew and Nari inside and got in last. She pulled the door shut. The vehicle rolled onto the buffer arterial and headed west.

Back in the building, the team was widening the search to the service lane. They'd find the north hatch unlocked. They'd read it. They'd start building the picture that told them which way the target had gone.

By the time they had the direction, the crew would be across three zones. By the time they had the destination, the direction wouldn't be enough.

Rook leaned back in the sealed compartment and looked at the ceiling and thought about nothing for forty-five seconds — the amount of time she had before the next thing needed her.

Forty-five seconds was enough.

The maintenance staircase opened behind a door in the east face of the Old Harbor waterfront building — a door you had to know was there before the lock mattered. Ekow found it by hand in the dark. He had come once in daylight to set his bearings, but every use after that had been at night, and the panel was built for night: flush with the ferrocement, the recessed handle invisible until you put your palm on the right stretch of wall and felt the inset.

The sequence was mechanical. Depress, rotate left, depress. No electronics. Old enough to predate the Syndicate's current access system, put in when the waterfront went up, back when the maintenance crews got one code and the people running the supply lines got another — the crews weren't meant to know what the lines carried.

Nobody had ever updated the assumption because nobody had needed to. It worked.

He had used this door eleven times. In his other life. Going the other way.

The staircase was fifteen steps down, narrow, ferrocement on both sides, close enough that his shoulders knew the walls without touching them. The smell of the canal reached them thirty meters before the canal did — enclosed water, old infrastructure, not clean but honest.

Scout's forward crawler ran point, its amber indicator the only light, the floor showing in a narrow ring and nothing past it.

Rook counted the steps. Fifteen down. The landing.

The covered walkway ran east along the canal embankment. Ceiling at two meters, the right side open to the water through iron grating — original, the bars four centimeters across, the coating worn to bare metal where four decades of GLMZ air had eaten at it. Not failing. Just worn to where the years showed. The water moved below the grating, dark, carrying the harbor's distant light on its surface without brightening.

At intervals, iron cable housings jutted from the embankment wall into the walkway. The supply lines for the Old Harbor community ran through here — power, civic data, water. The clinic on the corner drew auxiliary power off a conduit in this walkway. The market tied into the grid at a junction sixty meters east. The homes above the market had backup water through a pipe beside the cables. All of it behind the face of Old Harbor, below the market, in a corridor the community had no reason to know existed. The Syndicate had laid it in alongside the community's own infrastructure, slowly enough that the two were one thing now.

Boiler ducked once at a junction where the housings cut the clearance. He found the crouch on the second step and held it.

The diesel reached them at a hundred and fifty meters — old engine and enclosed water.

The dock showed at the corridor's end: covered, the roof four meters above the waterline, in good repair because it saw regular use. Iron mooring cleats. Iron chain guides. The hardware of a place built to move things quietly, and it had been moving them quietly for twelve years.

The boat was twelve meters, hull low, a maintenance-vessel build that read on the harbor grid as exactly what it claimed. GLMZ Harbor Services registration, a maintenance code, standard ID. The engine was old enough to hear and serviced enough to be steady — kept running because it was useful. The same logic the Syndicate ran on the community above them.

The operator stood at the mooring line. A woman in her fifties, weathered the way the harbor weathers the people who work it long enough that its light and air get into their skin. She looked at Ekow when the crew came out of the corridor.

Only at Ekow. She handed him the line and went to the controls without a word. The whole transaction was a look and a rope.

Rook set the crew by position — Boiler at the stern, Vox and Lace amidships, Scout at the starboard rail where her crawlers could reach the hull and the harbor perimeter.

Rook took the port midship and watched Nari board last, stepping from dock to hull with the steady movement of a body doing its task while the mind is somewhere else entirely.

Nari looked at the harbor.

The dock opened east. Past the cover: the canal mouth, the open harbor, the GLMZ at one in the morning. The Spine aeroplex at 480 meters throwing its light into the sky, the western shore reflected in the dark water. The lake — the same lake. From a Z3 diner window it was a thing you lived beside. From a maintenance dock at zero-hundred it was cold and close and the thing you were crossing to reach whatever came next.

Nari stood at the midship rail and watched it.

Ekow cast off and took the bow. The operator ran the boat off the dock in one clean move, no waste, the channel already in her hands. The hull cleared the mooring hardware. The dock fell back.

The boat moved east.

The silence came with it. Not arranged — the natural quiet of a crew after a job that had run from orderly to pressured to controlled in twelve minutes and was now done. Nobody had anything to add. Faking relief would have been a lie, and the crew didn't trade in those. The extraction was finished. The witness was aboard. Whatever needed working through would get worked through later, somewhere that allowed it.

Scout ran her crawlers along the hull, their lights showing at the waterline.

Boiler watched the way they'd come.

Vox had something low-priority on a thread, not surfaced.

Lace read the western shoreline the way she read any place she was getting ready to wear.

Ekow stood at the bow and did not turn around.

He had run this route eleven times the other way. Not this exact water, not this hour — the supply transfers went in daylight maintenance windows, the boat logged as scheduled work, the cargo listed as equipment. He had ridden it six of those eleven times, from a spot on the hull that wasn't the bow, because in the daytime the bow was the operator's. He knew the crossing took thirty-two minutes. He had counted it the first time and never needed to count again.

Some of what he had moved through this corridor was on the list. Not all of it — the route had carried things he never saw, before and after his part in it, the endless freight of an organization that ran its logistics through something that looked like something else. But some of it he knew by name. He had been there for the loading.

None of it could be taken back.

At the ten-minute mark the operator's hand came off the throttle and settled on a channel display Ekow couldn't read from the bow. The engine note dropped.

He turned then — the first time he'd turned all crossing — and saw what she'd seen: a second hull, low and dark, holding station across the outer channel where nothing was scheduled to be at this hour.

Harbor Services didn't patrol the outer approach at zero-hundred. Nothing did. The boat that was there had no running lights and no reason, and it sat directly on the standard channel line the operator had been about to take.

"Perimeter contact, starboard bow," Scout said, low, her crawler feed already on it.

"Stationary. No registration broadcast."

The operator looked at Ekow. Not at Rook — at Ekow. The look asked a question the rope hadn't: the channel was hers, but the choice of whether to hold the standard line or break for the slow inner cut was his, because he was the only one aboard who'd run this water enough to know what the inner cut cost.

The standard channel put them past the dark hull at forty meters, inside whatever it was watching for. The inner cut added distance and time and ran them shallow, over grating and old pilings that the harbor's shape had been keeping a secret for four decades — the kind of secret that stopped being one the moment a hull scraped it.

Ekow held the moment longer than he wanted to. He knew the crossing took thirty-two minutes. He had never once had to decide anything about it.

He put his hand flat on the rail and gave the operator the inner cut — two fingers angled off the standard line, the signal a maintenance crew used when a scheduled slot was fouled.

The operator took it without expression and brought the bow around, and the engine dropped again, and the boat that had carried this route toward the Lotus for twelve years went slow and shallow over water that had never been asked to hide anyone before.

The boat ran the inner harbor in twenty-two minutes and the outer in fourteen more.

Thirty-six, not the thirty-two the standard channel would have made it.

Nari didn't move from the rail for twenty-eight of them. She watched the water — the surface under the hull, the wake opening behind them and closing again as if the boat had never passed. Not working through it. Just watching, present somewhere without being anywhere in particular.

At twenty-eight minutes, she turned from the water and looked at Ekow's back.

He was still at the bow. Still not turned. She looked at him a long time, the way she had looked at him in the corridor outside her apartment — not recognition, not its absence either. Two people carrying similar weight in different shapes, each able to read it in the other by what it did to the posture, the stillness, the way a person holds a space when they aren't holding it lightly.

She looked at him, and he didn't turn, and after a while she looked back at the water.

Rook watched it and let it be.

The Z3 shoreline came up ahead. A commercial waterway access: covered berth, an overnight maintenance slot, the contact Vox had set two hours before.

The operator brought the hull in with the same economy she'd used leaving, and held the line while the crew stepped off in the order they'd boarded — Rook first, then Nari, then the others.

Ekow came off last.

He stood on the dock a moment after the others started toward the transit node. His back to the crew, he faced the open harbor past the canal mouth — the water, the lake, the distance. Not the way they'd come. The way that held the horizon.

He put his hand in his coat pocket. The hand stopped when it found what it was looking for. He didn't take anything out. It was the check — confirming a thing was still there without looking at it. Whatever was in the pocket had been there all night and would be there in the morning.

He took his hand out. He turned from the harbor and walked toward the transit node.

Rook had seen it from thirty meters. She looked away when he turned.

"Vehicle's staged," Vox said, in the channel.

"Z3 commercial registration, covered cargo, overnight route."

"We have it," Rook said.

The vehicle sat at the edge of the transit node, sealed compartment, the driver's face belonging to someone paid not to know what was in back. The crew loaded in. The door sealed. The vehicle moved north.

Old Harbor was behind them. The canal corridor closed. The dock was already logging the boat's departure as routine — a harbor services vessel finishing its overnight run, heading back to its mooring, nothing to note.

The Syndicate's supply line, run for twelve years to carry cargo toward the Lotus, had spent thirty-two minutes carrying one person away from them. Only the direction was different.

Nari sat with the bag in her lap, her hands on the strap, her eyes on the ceiling of the compartment.

She had been ready for three years for the night she'd board a boat at zero-hundred and cross the harbor.

She had packed carefully. She had imagined the leaving, the crossing, the moment of it.

She had not imagined the silence. You couldn't pack for that.

The vehicle ran north through the Z3 arterial. Eleven minutes to the safe house.

The GLMZ ran on around them at zero-one-thirty, doing what it always did — keeping the infrastructure up, keeping the services running, not asking who they were for.

The silence held the whole eleven minutes.

Nobody broke it. There was nothing to add.

At 0712, Nari said: "The administrative authorization has Seul-ki's direct signature."

Vox's pen stopped.

"That's not possible," she said. It came out of her before the crew's rule reasserted itself, and it landed hard in the room's careful quiet.

"I've read the Phase 0 authorization chain off the Headcount metadata. Twice. The signature block terminates at the Stem level. There's no Root-level mark on it. If Seul-ki signed, the data doesn't show it — which means either the data's been scrubbed, or the signature you're describing lives on a document I can't see and can't corroborate."

Nari held very still. The composure she'd carried all night stood at a new angle — not shaken, but confronted with a thing she hadn't accounted for.

"I saw it," she said. "The active version, 2211. Her mark was on the founding document's authorization page."

"Founding document, 2211," Vox said. "Not the Phase 0 deployment order. Those are eleven years apart. You're joining two things across eleven years from memory and one signature you saw once."

She set the pen down flat. "I'm not saying you're wrong. I'm saying the transcript now has a seam in it — the load-bearing name, the one that makes the whole thing prosecutable, rests on a record nobody can produce and metadata that actively contradicts it. If we build the operation on Seul-ki and the seam gives, we've built it on nothing."

The room, which had spent the night deciding not to interrupt, sat with the fact that interruption had finally been owed — and had come at the worst possible place, at the center Nari had saved for last.

Rook looked at the fourteen pages. Thirteen of them held. The fourteenth held the name that mattered, and now the fourteenth held a hole.

She said it in the same level voice as it was owed.
"Then we don't have it," she said.

She said the Root's name flat, without emphasis, nothing added to make it more than a name.

It was the name of the person who had signed off on the Phase 0 deployment and the founding document, who had run the Lotus Syndicate for forty years.

She named her last, not first, because she was the center, and you save the center for last.

Rook wrote it down. Then, beside it, she wrote a second word, smaller: *unconfirmed.*

Vox worked through the morning and into the afternoon. She didn't report her progress, and nobody asked her to. The crew ran on the patience of people who knew analysis finished when it finished, not when you wanted it to — asking Vox to report early was like asking Boiler for the load calculation before he'd run it. The result was worth the wait.

At 1634, she looked up from the equipment.

"I need the table," she said.

Rook cleared it. The crew moved their chairs. Vox spread four sheets of paper across the surface — not a display, physical paper with handwritten analysis and printed data extracts she'd pulled from the gray-market archives and marked up. Each sheet carried a color in the margin, the four-color code she'd used since the rebuild.

She had run four channels since the graze wound. Not because the rebuild stopped there — she could have gone wider. She had chosen four, because four was the number where information came in ordered instead of by the ton, where she processed what she got instead of chasing what she missed. She'd made the choice on purpose and never revisited it. This analysis had come through four channels, and in eleven hours it had produced a connection she was sure was structural, not chance.

She pointed to the first sheet.

"The Headcount data layer," she said. "Fifteen years of Gray Zone demographics in civic-grid schema. The schema is GLMZ Census Infrastructure Version 4.2 — the census schema from 2211. That's the year the GLMZ retired the heritage classification field from the public census, under the Meridian Charter's data equity provisions." A pause. "They took it out of the public record. They didn't take it out of the underlying database. They reclassified it as restricted infrastructure data. The infrastructure version of the schema still carries it."

She let it sit.

"Headcount is collected in the infrastructure version," she said. "Not the public one. It has the heritage field. Fifteen years of heritage classification for the Z4-through-Z7 corridor — migration, household composition, neuretics registration status, debt distribution by heritage. Everything the public census stopped counting in 2211."

"Fifteen years," Ekow said. He said it like a man who had already run the sum and gotten the same answer again.

"The oldest entry is dated two months after the Charter retired the field," Vox said. "The sculpture starts the day the public record stops."

She moved to the second sheet. "That's what the founding document calls the demographic baseline — a registry that can exist without authorization, because it's built around a field the official record no longer admits it keeps."

Scout said, "The sculpture was built for this."

"Yes," Vox said. "It wasn't commissioned to document Gray Zone demographics. It was commissioned to build the Protocol's first Phase 1 requirement. The art commission was the collection vehicle."

She looked at the four sheets. "The commission went to the Gray Horizon Collective in 2213 — a Z4 art and civic documentation group. It wouldn't have looked strange to them. The brief specified the data format: civic-grid schema, structured data built into a physical sculpture, public installation in a Z3 collector's market."

"Publicly visible?" Rook asked.

"A public installation muddies the ownership question," Vox said. "If the data is public — if anyone can read the sculpture's data layer — it isn't a private registry. It's a public artwork with accessible data. The Charter's privacy provisions don't touch public artworks the way they touch private databases." A pause. "The sculpture is both. Publicly available, privately useful. The data is there for anyone who can read it. The only people who can read it the way it's meant to be read are the people who commissioned it."

Lace looked at the table. "A whole data-collection method dressed up as public art," she said, and her voice had a quality Rook didn't hear from her often — not quite contempt, something more exact. A woman who had spent a career being the only one in the room who saw the gap between the face and the fact, now looking at an institution that had worked that gap at scale, for decades.

"The same method," Lace said. "At a different size."

"Yes," Vox said.

She moved to the third sheet.

"Second component: neuretics registry access." She had an extract from the Z6 test cluster's admin logs — gray-market, from an archive she wasn't going to name. "Phase 0. Six months. The 200,000 Z6 records now in Lotus hands. Confirmed by Nari and by the log signature." She pointed to a line. "It ran through a Civic Systems Engineer. Grade 4. The access needed a Grade 4's credentials — the neuretics infrastructure is Grade 4 minimum for direct registry queries, per the Charter's technical rules."

"They coerced someone?" Rook asked.

"That's the read," Vox said. "The test deployment needed a Grade 4 engineer's cooperation over and over, for six months. That's not a single breach. Someone inside the infrastructure was feeding it." She pointed to the fourth sheet. "The deployment window. The generation update. Thirty days out. During the update, Article 14 review is suspended. If the full registry backdoor — not the test cluster, the full grid — is in Lotus hands before the update starts, they run the complete Phase 1 query set during the window, and the results are delivered before review comes back online."

She stopped. She looked at the four sheets, then at the crew.

"Three components," she said. "Demographic baseline: the Headcount sculpture, in our possession. Second: neuretics registry access — the Syndicate is acquiring it, through a coerced engineer, with full deployment timed to the generation update. Third: the window itself. Thirty days."

The room went quiet in a way different from the quiet after Nari's briefing — denser, more operational. Nari's briefing had been the weight of knowing what the Protocol was. This was the weight of knowing what it needed and which pieces the Syndicate already held.

"We have the first component," Rook said.

Lace didn't let it stand clean. "Say the second and third the way you're saying them," she said. "The engineer's coerced — where's that from? A log signature and a grade minimum. That's inference. And the thirty days — the update's public record, fine, but the delivery timed to it is your read, not the founding document's word. I've seen files this tidy before. Tidy is what a plant looks like."

Vox's jaw set. "The signature isn't inference. The same credential runs the same query set across six months of logs. One engineer, repeated, on infrastructure that resets access per session. That doesn't happen without a standing arrangement. Coercion is the read. The standing arrangement is the fact."

"And if the archive you won't name is the arrangement?" Lace said. "Gray-market logs are cheap to write. You built three components off two of them. If the second's cooked, the whole shape is cooked, and we've been reading a story someone wanted us to read since the Z3 market."

The four sheets sat between them, and for a moment nobody moved to defend either one.

It was Boiler who broke it, quietly — not taking Lace's side, taking the crack she'd opened. "If it's a plant, it's a plant that predicted us taking the sculpture. That's a lot of engineering for a story." But he said it slow, and it didn't close the question. It widened it.

Vox looked at her own analysis differently for the first time in eleven hours — not at whether it connected, but at whether it could be made to connect by someone who wanted it to.

She didn't have the answer.

That was the disaster: the shape held, and she could no longer be sure the shape was hers.

"Who organized the collection?" Ekow asked.

"The brief came from a Z5 cultural development foundation," Vox said. "Established 2212 — one year before the commission. Three directors on the incorporation papers. Two have no other affiliations. The third is a former Stem assistant."

Nobody asked her to confirm the link. It was confirmed.

"How long did we have the sculpture before the Thorn team flagged us?" Scout asked.

"Park was watching it for three weeks before we arrived," Vox said. "He clocked our entry and exit. He didn't move."

"Because moving would have cost them the sculpture," Rook said. "They needed us to take it first. They were waiting for the recovery so they could recover it from us."

She ran the timeline in her head. The Thorns reached Nari's building at 2347. The market run had been three days before that. Three days between the recovery and the approach on Nari. Not chance.

"Park flagged us at the market," she said. "The deployment on Nari was three days later. He ran a cross-reference."

"Adaeze's referral came through his network," Ekow said. "Not directly. But the chain exists — anyone looking at the right links could map the route from Adaeze's legal cooperative to the crew she'd call." He said it flat, describing how Lotus intelligence worked when it worked.

"He moved on Nari when he identified the crew," Rook said. "Not because the sculpture triggered it — because the crew that took the sculpture was also the crew tied to Adaeze, who was tied to Nari. He wasn't chasing the sculpture. He was chasing whoever came for it."

The four sheets were stacked on the table. Vox put her equipment on standby.

The safe house was quiet. The uneven table. The mismatched chairs. The backup light running its steady, inadequate output — enough and nothing more.

Three components. One theirs. One being built by the organization they'd been unknowingly working for. One a countdown running whether they acted or not.

"Thirty days," Boiler said. He said it the way he said structural facts — no fatalism, no alarm, just the result. "If the generation update starts in thirty days and the full registry access is delivered before it starts, the Phase 1 query runs during the window before review comes back."

"Yes," Vox said.

"And the Phase 0 data — the 200,000 Z6 records — is available now," he said. "Combined with the Headcount baseline."

"Enough to begin targeted displacement in Z6 immediately," Vox said. "Before Phase 1. The Phase 0 set is smaller, but it covers Z6 completely. They don't need Phase 1 to start in Z6. Phase 1 just widens it to the whole Z4-through-Z7 corridor."

Boiler looked at the table. At nothing on it in particular — the look of a man running something in his head that wasn't a structural calculation. He didn't say what it was. He didn't need to.

"We need to think about what comes next," Rook said.

Nobody disagreed.

The afternoon light showed at the top of the basement window — the Z3 street in its late-day rhythm, the city doing what it always did outside of basements and harbor corridors and crawlways. Keeping the infrastructure up, keeping the services running, not asking what was underneath.

Vox put her four sheets in the operational file.

The Headcount sculpture sat in the sealed cargo case in the corner. The first component of a plan forty years in the making, in a Z3 safe house, in the hands of the crew the plan's authors had paid to retrieve it.

The lines were drawn. Vox had drawn them. The crew had watched them connect — and Lace had put a finger on one of them and asked whether it had been drawn for them to find.

Rook stood at the table with the four sheets in front of her, the crew seated, and looked at the window a moment. Not through it — the basement window showed the Z3 street at ankle height, the late-afternoon traffic passing in fragments of shoes and wheels.

She wasn't seeing the street. She was seeing the shape of the thing.

Then she turned to the crew.

"We're in a genocide intervention," she said.

She said it plainly. It didn't need softening and she wasn't going to soften it. Across three jobs with this crew, and whatever she'd been before them, she'd learned that lies about what a thing was tended to fail at the worst moment.
